Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well… I guess I came in because I’ve been feeling uneasy, even when things are supposed to be normal. Like at church, or with the walking club—I used to enjoy that, you know, it’s nice to get out and see people, and they have those little bulletins at church with all the announcements and birthdays and such—but lately I can’t shake the feeling that some of them are watching me.

And not just watching, exactly… it feels like they’re putting little messages about me in what they say, or in the bulletin. I know that may sound odd, but it keeps happening in my mind, and it’s been bothering me.

Well, I still live at home, so I manage some things… I can make tea and toast, that sort of thing. But I’m not keeping up like I used to. I remember thinking it started around when the weather was changing, but then I get mixed up because that was when my neighbor was fixing her porch too, and I kept hearing all that hammering… but yes, about eleven months.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to express your thoughts clearly or in an orderly fashion when talking to others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I try to answer plain, but then I’ll start telling someone about the mail, because maybe there was a flyer or something from church, and then somehow I’m talking about quilt patterns, or those little birds that come to the feeder, and then—well, I lose the straight line of it.\n\nI can see it on people’s faces sometimes, like they’re waiting for me to get back to the point, and I’m not even sure what point I was making. It’s embarrassing, because in my head it feels connected, but when it comes out, it gets all tangled.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any problems with your daily activities, like cooking, cleaning, or taking care of yourself?\n\nPatient: Well, I still live at home, so I manage some things… I can make tea and toast, that sort of thing. But I’m not keeping up like I used to. I used to go to my quilting group—there’s a lady there who always brings lemon bars, and she cuts the fabric so straight it makes me jealous—but I haven’t been going much.\n\nAnd my walking club, I sort of fell away from that too. The garden’s been getting away from me, which bothers me because I always liked my flowers. My daughter’s been checking in more now, making sure I’ve eaten something decent and reminding me about appointments. I don’t like needing that, but… yes, things have slipped some.\n\nDoctor: Have you been acting in ways that seem strange or unusual to other people?\n\nPatient: Yes… maybe. I don’t mean to be odd, but there are mornings where I’ll be standing by the front door with my purse in my hand, just… standing there. Like I was going somewhere, but I don’t go out, and I can’t quite remember what I meant to do.\n\nSometimes I notice the light coming through the little window there, and I start thinking I should wash the curtain, because it gets dusty near the door, and then I’m still holding the purse. So yes, I suppose if someone saw me, it would look strange.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It’s not all the time, but it does shake me up when it happens.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Oh… I’d say it’s been going on for maybe eleven months now. I try to answer plain, but then I’ll start telling someone about the mail, because maybe there was a flyer or something from church, and then somehow I’m talking about quilt patterns, or those little birds that come to the feeder, and then—well, I lose the straight line of it.\n\nI can see it on people’s faces sometimes, like they’re waiting for me to get back to the point, and I’m not even sure what point I was making. Yes… it has, I suppose. I still live at home, I’m not needing to be somewhere else or anything, but I don’t keep up with things like I used to.

I try to answer plain, but then I’ll start telling someone about the mail, because maybe there was a flyer or something from church, and then somehow I’m talking about quilt patterns, or those little birds that come to the feeder, and then—well, I lose the straight line of it.\n\nI can see it on people’s faces sometimes, like they’re waiting for me to get back to the point, and I’m not even sure what point I was making. It’s embarrassing, because in my head it feels connected, but when it comes out, it gets all tangled.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any problems with your daily activities, like cooking, cleaning, or taking care of yourself?\n\nPatient: Well, I still live at home, so I manage some things… I can make tea and toast, that sort of thing. house is quiet I hear my name or a few words in my friend's voice from the kitchen or porch, clear enough that I answer before I remember nobody is there.\n- key: S021\n  name: Disorganized_Thinking_Speech\n  description: Symptoms where speech or thought appears scattered and difficult to follow due to a lack of logical or consistent flow.\n  manifestation: When I try to explain things, I start with one thought about the mail or church and end up talking about quilt patterns, birds, and warnings, and then I can see people don't know what I'm getting at.\n- key: S022\n  name: Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior\n  description: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.\n  manifestation: There are mornings when I find myself standing by the front door with my purse in my hand for a long time, not going out and not really knowing what I was about to do.\n- key: S023\n  name: Negative_Symptoms\n  description: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.\n  manifestation: I don't talk much at coffee hour anymore, and even when someone tells a funny story I just sit there with a flat face like the feeling can't get out.\n- key: S025\n  name: Loss_of_Interest\n  description: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.\n  manifestation: I used to love my garden, quilting, and reading before bed, but now I mostly leave the quilt basket untouched and the weeds can grow without me caring much.\n- key: S026\n  name: Weight_Appetite_Change\n  description: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.\n  manifestation: Most days food just doesn't appeal to me, so I pick at toast or soup and my skirts have been hanging looser than they used to.\n- key: S029\n  name: Fatigue\n  description: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.\n  manifestation: Even after a full night in bed, carrying laundry or walking to the mailbox can make my body feel heavy, like I've already done a day's work.\n- key: S030\n  name: Worthlessness_Guilt\n  description: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).\n  manifestation: I keep going over every little thing I might have done wrong, especially not calling my friend more, and I feel like I must have failed her and everybody else.\n- key: S032\n  name: Death_Suicide\n  description: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.\n  manifestation: I don't have a plan to hurt myself, but I catch myself thinking more and more that it might be all right if I went to sleep and didn't wake up.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: psychotic_symptoms and depressive_episode occur concurrently\n  manifestation: When I was at my lowest, barely eating and thinking I had failed everyone, I was also hearing my friend's voice and believing church people were sending messages about me.\n- key: additional_requirement_2\n  name: Additional Requirement 2\n  description: depressive_episode is present for the majority of the total duration of the active and residual portions of the illness (Criterion C)\n  manifestation: For most of this whole stretch, the sadness, guilt, and loss of interest have been there in the background, even during times when the voices or suspicious thoughts were quieter.\n- key: additional_requirement_3\n  name: Additional Requirement 3\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur for 2 or more weeks in the absence of a major mood episode (Criterion B)\n  manifestation: There was a spell of several weeks when I was getting dressed, eating a bit better, and not feeling so weighed down, but I still heard my name in the house and thought people at church were hinting about me.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I still live at home, but I have stopped keeping up with quilting gatherings, walking club, and much of my gardening, and my daughter has started checking on my meals and appointments more often.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n start. Like I’ll be talking about the mail, or maybe someone from church I meant to call, and then it goes sideways somehow—quilt patterns, the blue one, the birds outside, and then those warnings I’ve noticed.\n\nAnd I can see it on their face after a bit. They’re listening, but they don’t really know what I’m getting at. Sometimes I don’t either, once I hear myself.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been causing you significant distress or problems in your daily life, such as at work, with friends, or with family?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has, I suppose. I still live at home, I’m not needing to be somewhere else or anything, but I don’t keep up with things like I used to.\n\nI stopped going to the quilting gatherings mostly, and the walking club too… which is a shame, because there was this one lady who always had the nicest little thermos, red with flowers on it—anyway. And my garden, I used to be out there fussing with it more, but now it’s just… not as much.\n\nMy daughter’s been checking on me more too, about whether I ate and if I remembered appointments. I don’t like feeling watched over, but I guess she’s worried.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where your symptoms seemed to disappear or get much better, only to come back later?\n\nPatient: Not really disappeared, no… I mean, maybe some days feel a little quieter than others, like if the weather’s nice or I’ve slept better, but it’s been there more or less since not long after my close friend died.\n\nThat was about… a year and five months ago now, I think. Funny how I can remember that, but then I’ll forget where I put the little address book with her handwriting in it—well, anyway.   {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF20.0, F23\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I came in because I’ve been feeling uneasy, even when things are supposed to be normal. Like at church, or with the walking club—I used to enjoy that, you know, it’s nice to get out and see people, and they have those little bulletins at church with all the announcements and birthdays and such—but lately I can’t shake the feeling that some of them are watching me.\n\nAnd not just watching, exactly… it feels like they’re putting little messages about me in what they say, or in the bulletin. I know that may sound odd, but it keeps happening in my mind, and it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or sounds that other people can't hear?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes… mostly when the house is real quiet. I’ll be in the living room, or doing dishes, and I’ll hear my name, or just a few words, like it’s my friend calling from the kitchen or out on the porch.\n\nIt sounds clear enough that I’ve actually answered back before, and then I remember nobody’s there. It’s not all the time, but it does shake me up when it happens.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Oh… I’d say it’s been going on for maybe eleven months now. Not every single minute, but the voices and those little messages, and even just trying to keep my thoughts lined up… it’s been most of this past year. I remember thinking it started around when the weather was changing, but then I get mixed up because that was when my neighbor was fixing her porch too, and I kept hearing all that hammering… but yes, about eleven months.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to express your thoughts clearly or in an orderly fashion when talking to others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I try to answer plain, but then I’ll start telling someone about the mail, because maybe there was a flyer or something from church, and then somehow I’m talking about quilt patterns, or those little birds that come to the feeder, and then—well, I lose the straight line of it.\n\nI can see it on people’s faces sometimes, like they’re waiting for me to get back to the point, and I’m not even sure what point I was making. It’s embarrassing, because in my head it feels connected, but when it comes out, it gets all tangled.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any problems with your daily activities, like cooking, cleaning, or taking care of yourself?\n\nPatient: Well, I still live at home, so I manage some things… I can make tea and toast, that sort of thing. But I’m not keeping up like I used to. I used to go to my quilting group—there’s a lady there who always brings lemon bars, and she cuts the fabric so straight it makes me jealous—but I haven’t been going much.\n\nAnd my walking club, I sort of fell away from that too. The garden’s been getting away from me, which bothers me because I always liked my flowers. My daughter’s been checking in more now, making sure I’ve eaten something decent and reminding me about appointments. I don’t like needing that, but… yes, things have slipped some.\n\nDoctor: Have you been acting in ways that seem strange or unusual to other people?\n\nPatient: Yes… maybe. I don’t mean to be odd, but there are mornings where I’ll be standing by the front door with my purse in my hand, just… standing there. Like I was going somewhere, but I don’t go out, and I can’t quite remember what I meant to do.\n\nSometimes I notice the light coming through the little window there, and I start thinking I should wash the curtain, because it gets dusty near the door, and then I’m still holding the purse. So yes, I suppose if someone saw me, it would look strange.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where you became immobile or unable to move, like being stuck in one position for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that happens some. Like I said, there are mornings I end up standing by the front door with my purse in my hand, and I’m just there for a long while. Not exactly frozen like a statue, but not moving on either.\n\nI’ll think, “Was I going to the store?” or maybe I meant to check the mail, and then I notice the rug needs shaking out, or there’s a smudge on the little glass pane, and I’m still just standing there. It’s a strange feeling, like the next step got misplaced.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to stay focused on one topic when talking or thinking?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I start trying to explain one thing, like whether I got the mail or if I meant to call someone from church, and then somehow I’m talking about quilt patterns—because the blue one really does remind me of those little birds near the feeder—and then I’ll mention the warnings I’ve been noticing, and… well, by then I can see people looking at me like they don’t know where I’m going with it.\n\nI don’t always notice it right away. I think I’m making sense, but it comes out all tangled.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your thoughts are often disjointed or difficult to follow, even when you're not talking to someone?\n\nPatient: Yes… even when I’m just sitting by myself, it can be that way. I’ll start thinking, “Did I bring the mail in?” or maybe I should call someone from church, and then somehow my mind is on the quilt in the back room, the blue pattern, and those birds outside… and then the warnings I’ve been noticing sort of come into it.\n\nIt feels connected to me at the time, but if I tried to say it out loud, people would look at me like they don’t know what I’m getting at. Sometimes I don’t know how I got from one thought to the other either.\n\nDoctor: When you're talking to someone and your thoughts seem to jump around, do you think the other person can usually understand what you're trying to say?\n\nPatient: Not usually, no… I mean, I think I’m explaining it clear enough when I start. Like I’ll be talking about the mail, or maybe someone from church I meant to call, and then it goes sideways somehow—quilt patterns, the blue one, the birds outside, and then those warnings I’ve noticed.\n\nAnd I can see it on their face after a bit. They’re listening, but they don’t really know what I’m getting at. Sometimes I don’t either, once I hear myself.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been causing you significant distress or problems in your daily life, such as at work, with friends, or with family?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has, I suppose. I still live at home, I’m not needing to be somewhere else or anything, but I don’t keep up with things like I used to.\n\nI stopped going to the quilting gatherings mostly, and the walking club too… which is a shame, because there was this one lady who always had the nicest little thermos, red with flowers on it—anyway. And my garden, I used to be out there fussing with it more, but now it’s just… not as much.\n\nMy daughter’s been checking on me more too, about whether I ate and if I remembered appointments. I don’t like feeling watched over, but I guess she’s worried.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where your symptoms seemed to disappear or get much better, only to come back later?\n\nPatient: Not really disappeared, no… I mean, maybe some days feel a little quieter than others, like if the weather’s nice or I’ve slept better, but it’s been there more or less since not long after my close friend died.\n\nThat was about… a year and five months ago now, I think. Funny how I can remember that, but then I’ll forget where I put the little address book with her handwriting in it—well, anyway. It started after that time, and I don’t think it’s really gone away and come back.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been present all the time since they started, or have they come and gone?\n\nPatient: It’s been more all the time, I think… not really coming and going. Since not long after my close friend died—about a year and five months ago now—it’s just sort of stayed around.\n\nSome days are softer than others, like if I keep busy with small things, folding towels or looking through old fabric scraps… but no, I wouldn’t say it left and then came back.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I came in because I’ve been feeling uneasy, even when things are supposed to be normal. Like at church, or with the walking club—I used to enjoy that, you know, it’s nice to get out and see people, and they have those little bulletins at church with all the announcements and birthdays and such—but lately I can’t shake the feeling that some of them are watching me.\n\nAnd not just watching, exactly… it feels like they’re putting little messages about me in what they say, or in the bulletin. I know that may sound odd, but it keeps happening in my mind, and it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or sounds that other people can't hear?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes… mostly when the house is real quiet. I’ll be in the living room, or doing dishes, and I’ll hear my name, or just a few words, like it’s my friend calling from the kitchen or out on the porch.\n\nIt sounds clear enough that I’ve actually answered back before, and then I remember nobody’s there. It’s not all the time, but it does shake me up when it happens.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Oh… I’d say it’s been going on for maybe eleven months now. Not every single minute, but the voices and those little messages, and even just trying to keep my thoughts lined up… it’s been most of this past year. I remember thinking it started around when the weather was changing, but then I get mixed up because that was when my neighbor was fixing her porch too, and I kept hearing all that hammering… but yes, about eleven months.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to express your thoughts clearly or in an orderly fashion when talking to others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I try to answer plain, but then I’ll start telling someone about the mail, because maybe there was a flyer or something from church, and then somehow I’m talking about quilt patterns, or those little birds that come to the feeder, and then—well, I lose the straight line of it.\n\nI can see it on people’s faces sometimes, like they’re waiting for me to get back to the point, and I’m not even sure what point I was making. It’s embarrassing, because in my head it feels connected, but when it comes out, it gets all tangled.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any problems with your daily activities, like cooking, cleaning, or taking care of yourself?\n\nPatient: Well, I still live at home, so I manage some things… I can make tea and toast, that sort of thing. But I’m not keeping up like I used to. I used to go to my quilting group—there’s a lady there who always brings lemon bars, and she cuts the fabric so straight it makes me jealous—but I haven’t been going much.\n\nAnd my walking club, I sort of fell away from that too. The garden’s been getting away from me, which bothers me because I always liked my flowers. My daughter’s been checking in more now, making sure I’ve eaten something decent and reminding me about appointments. I don’t like needing that, but… yes, things have slipped some.\n\nDoctor: Have you been acting in ways that seem strange or unusual to other people?\n\nPatient: Yes… maybe. I don’t mean to be odd, but there are mornings where I’ll be standing by the front door with my purse in my hand, just… standing there. Like I was going somewhere, but I don’t go out, and I can’t quite remember what I meant to do.\n\nSometimes I notice the light coming through the little window there, and I start thinking I should wash the curtain, because it gets dusty near the door, and then I’m still holding the purse. So yes, I suppose if someone saw me, it would look strange.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where you became immobile or unable to move, like being stuck in one position for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that happens some. Like I said, there are mornings I end up standing by the front door with my purse in my hand, and I’m just there for a long while. Not exactly frozen like a statue, but not moving on either.\n\nI’ll think, “Was I going to the store?” or maybe I meant to check the mail, and then I notice the rug needs shaking out, or there’s a smudge on the little glass pane, and I’m still just standing there. It’s a strange feeling, like the next step got misplaced.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to stay focused on one topic when talking or thinking?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I start trying to explain one thing, like whether I got the mail or if I meant to call someone from church, and then somehow I’m talking about quilt patterns—because the blue one really does remind me of those little birds near the feeder—and then I’ll mention the warnings I’ve been noticing, and… well, by then I can see people looking at me like they don’t know where I’m going with it.\n\nI don’t always notice it right away. I think I’m making sense, but it comes out all tangled.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your thoughts are often disjointed or difficult to follow, even when you're not talking to someone?\n\nPatient: Yes… even when I’m just sitting by myself, it can be that way. I’ll start thinking, “Did I bring the mail in?” or maybe I should call someone from church, and then somehow my mind is on the quilt in the back room, the blue pattern, and those birds outside… and then the warnings I’ve been noticing sort of come into it.\n\nIt feels connected to me at the time, but if I tried to say it out loud, people would look at me like they don’t know what I’m getting at. Sometimes I don’t know how I got from one thought to the other either.\n\nDoctor: When you're talking to someone and your thoughts seem to jump around, do you think the other person can usually understand what you're trying to say?\n\nPatient: Not usually, no… I mean, I think I’m explaining it clear enough when I start. The garden’s been getting away from me, which bothers me because I always liked my flowers. My daughter’s been checking in more now, making sure I’ve eaten something decent and reminding me about appointments. I don’t like needing that, but… yes, things have slipped some.\n\nDoctor: Have you been acting in ways that seem strange or unusual to other people?\n\nPatient: Yes… maybe. I don’t mean to be odd, but there are mornings where I’ll be standing by the front door with my purse in my hand, just… standing there. Like I was going somewhere, but I don’t go out, and I can’t quite remember what I meant to do.\n\nSometimes I notice the light coming through the little window there, and I start thinking I should wash the curtain, because it gets dusty near the door, and then I’m still holding the purse. So yes, I suppose if someone saw me, it would look strange.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where you became immobile or unable to move, like being stuck in one position for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that happens some. Like I said, there are mornings I end up standing by the front door with my purse in my hand, and I’m just there for a long while. Not exactly frozen like a statue, but not moving on either.\n\nI’ll think, “Was I going to the store?” or maybe I meant to check the mail, and then I notice the rug needs shaking out, or there’s a smudge on the little glass pane, and I’m still just standing there. It’s a strange feeling, like the next step got misplaced.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to stay focused on one topic when talking or thinking?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I start trying to explain one thing, like whether I got the mail or if I meant to call someone from church, and then somehow I’m talking about quilt patterns—because the blue one really does remind me of those little birds near the feeder—and then I’ll mention the warnings I’ve been noticing, and… well, by then I can see people looking at me like they don’t know where I’m going with it.\n\nI don’t always notice it right away. I think I’m making sense, but it comes out all tangled.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your thoughts are often disjointed or difficult to follow, even when you're not talking to someone?\n\nPatient: Yes… even when I’m just sitting by myself, it can be that way. I’ll start thinking, “Did I bring the mail in?” or maybe I should call someone from church, and then somehow my mind is on the quilt in the back room, the blue pattern, and those birds outside… and then the warnings I’ve been noticing sort of come into it.\n\nIt feels connected to me at the time, but if I tried to say it out loud, people would look at me like they don’t know what I’m getting at. Sometimes I don’t know how I got from one thought to the other either.\n\nDoctor: When you're talking to someone and your thoughts seem to jump around, do you think the other person can usually understand what you're trying to say?\n\nPatient: Not usually, no… I mean, I think I’m explaining it clear enough when I start. Like I’ll be talking about the mail, or maybe someone from church I meant to call, and then it goes sideways somehow—quilt patterns, the blue one, the birds outside, and then those warnings I’ve noticed.\n\nAnd I can see it on their face after a bit. They’re listening, but they don’t really know what I’m getting at. Sometimes I don’t either, once I hear myself.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been causing you significant distress or problems in your daily life, such as at work, with friends, or with family?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has, I suppose. I still live at home, I’m not needing to be somewhere else or anything, but I don’t keep up with things like I used to.\n\nI stopped going to the quilting gatherings mostly, and the walking club too… which is a shame, because there was this one lady who always had the nicest little thermos, red with flowers on it—anyway. And my garden, I used to be out there fussing with it more, but now it’s just… not as much.\n\nMy daughter’s been checking on me more too, about whether I ate and if I remembered appointments. I don’t like feeling watched over, but I guess she’s worried.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where your symptoms seemed to disappear or get much better, only to come back later?\n\nPatient: Not really disappeared, no… I mean, maybe some days feel a little quieter than others, like if the weather’s nice or I’ve slept better, but it’s been there more or less since not long after my close friend died.\n\nThat was about… a year and five months ago now, I think. Funny how I can remember that, but then I’ll forget where I put the little address book with her handwriting in it—well, anyway. It started after that time, and I don’t think it’s really gone away and come back.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been present all the time since they started, or have they come and gone?\n\nPatient: It’s been more all the time, I think… not really coming and going. Since not long after my close friend died—about a year and five months ago now—it’s just sort of stayed around.\n\nSome days are softer than others, like if I keep busy with small things, folding towels or looking through old fabric scraps… but no, I wouldn’t say it left and then came back.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble organizing your thoughts in your head, even when you're not trying to explain them to someone else?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think so. It’s like I’ll start thinking about one thing, like whether I brought in the mail, or if I was meant to call someone from church, and then somehow I’m thinking about quilt patterns—those little flying geese ones—and then birds outside, and then… warnings, or what I was supposed to be careful about.\n\nAnd when I try to say it out loud, I can see people’s faces change a little, like they’re waiting for the point and I’ve lost the thread. I don’t always notice it right away, but then I feel foolish.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.     "content": "Return the JSON now."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
She was… she was my closest friend, really. And after she died I just kept turning it over and over in my head, like a stone in my pocket. I think about whether I should’ve called her more, or gone by more often, or noticed something I was supposed to notice.

I know people say, “You did what you could,” but I don’t feel that way. I feel like I failed her somehow. And then it spreads, you know… like if I failed her, maybe I’ve failed everybody else too. My family, people from church… even little things, like forgetting to send a card, start feeling like proof.

I’m sorry, I’m getting off track. She used to like those lemon cookies from the bakery on Maple—well, I don’t know if it’s still there now. But yes… mostly it left me feeling like I did something wrong, or didn’t do enough, and I keep going back over it.

I think… maybe the trouble is worse when I try to say it out loud. I know that may sound odd, but it keeps happening in my mind, and it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or sounds that other people can't hear?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes… mostly when the house is real quiet. I’ll be in the living room, or doing dishes, and I’ll hear my name, or just a few words, like it’s my friend calling from the kitchen or out on the porch.\n\nIt sounds clear enough that I’ve actually answered back before, and then I remember nobody’s there. It’s not all the time, but it does shake me up when it happens.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Oh… I’d say it’s been going on for maybe eleven months now. Not every single minute, but the voices and those little messages, and even just trying to keep my thoughts lined up… it’s been most of this past year. I remember thinking it started around when the weather was changing, but then I get mixed up because that was when my neighbor was fixing her porch too, and I kept hearing all that hammering… but yes, about eleven months.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to express your thoughts clearly or in an orderly fashion when talking to others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I try to answer plain, but then I’ll start telling someone about the mail, because maybe there was a flyer or something from church, and then somehow I’m talking about quilt patterns, or those little birds that come to the feeder, and then—well, I lose the straight line of it.\n\nI can see it on people’s faces sometimes, like they’re waiting for me to get back to the point, and I’m not even sure what point I was making. It’s embarrassing, because in my head it feels connected, but when it comes out, it gets all tangled.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any problems with your daily activities, like cooking, cleaning, or taking care of yourself?\n\nPatient: Well, I still live at home, so I manage some things… I can make tea and toast, that sort of thing. But I’m not keeping up like I used to. I used to go to my quilting group—there’s a lady there who always brings lemon bars, and she cuts the fabric so straight it makes me jealous—but I haven’t been going much.\n\nAnd my walking club, I sort of fell away from that too. The garden’s been getting away from me, which bothers me because I always liked my flowers. My daughter’s been checking in more now, making sure I’ve eaten something decent and reminding me about appointments. I don’t like needing that, but… yes, things have slipped some.\n\nDoctor: Have you been acting in ways that seem strange or unusual to other people?\n\nPatient: Yes… maybe. I don’t mean to be odd, but there are mornings where I’ll be standing by the front door with my purse in my hand, just… standing there. Like I was going somewhere, but I don’t go out, and I can’t quite remember what I meant to do.\n\nSometimes I notice the light coming through the little window there, and I start thinking I should wash the curtain, because it gets dusty near the door, and then I’m still holding the purse. So yes, I suppose if someone saw me, it would look strange.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where you became immobile or unable to move, like being stuck in one position for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that happens some. Like I said, there are mornings I end up standing by the front door with my purse in my hand, and I’m just there for a long while. Not exactly frozen like a statue, but not moving on either.\n\nI’ll think, “Was I going to the store?” or maybe I meant to check the mail, and then I notice the rug needs shaking out, or there’s a smudge on the little glass pane, and I’m still just standing there. It’s a strange feeling, like the next step got misplaced.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to stay focused on one topic when talking or thinking?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I start trying to explain one thing, like whether I got the mail or if I meant to call someone from church, and then somehow I’m talking about quilt patterns—because the blue one really does remind me of those little birds near the feeder—and then I’ll mention the warnings I’ve been noticing, and… well, by then I can see people looking at me like they don’t know where I’m going with it.\n\nI don’t always notice it right away. I think I’m making sense, but it comes out all tangled.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your thoughts are often disjointed or difficult to follow, even when you're not talking to someone?\n\nPatient: Yes… even when I’m just sitting by myself, it can be that way. I’ll start thinking, “Did I bring the mail in?” or maybe I should call someone from church, and then somehow my mind is on the quilt in the back room, the blue pattern, and those birds outside… and then the warnings I’ve been noticing sort of come into it.\n\nIt feels connected to me at the time, but if I tried to say it out loud, people would look at me like they don’t know what I’m getting at. Sometimes I don’t know how I got from one thought to the other either.\n\nDoctor: When you're talking to someone and your thoughts seem to jump around, do you think the other person can usually understand what you're trying to say?\n\nPatient: Not usually, no… I mean, I think I’m explaining it clear enough when I start. Like I’ll be talking about the mail, or maybe someone from church I meant to call, and then it goes sideways somehow—quilt patterns, the blue one, the birds outside, and then those warnings I’ve noticed.\n\nAnd I can see it on their face after a bit. They’re listening, but they don’t really know what I’m getting at. Sometimes I don’t either, once I hear myself.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been causing you significant distress or problems in your daily life, such as at work, with friends, or with family?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has, I suppose. I still live at home, I’m not needing to be somewhere else or anything, but I don’t keep up with things like I used to.\n\nI stopped going to the quilting gatherings mostly, and the walking club too… which is a shame, because there was this one lady who always had the nicest little thermos, red with flowers on it—anyway. And my garden, I used to be out there fussing with it more, but now it’s just… not as much.\n\nMy daughter’s been checking on me more too, about whether I ate and if I remembered appointments. I don’t like feeling watched over, but I guess she’s worried.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where your symptoms seemed to disappear or get much better, only to come back later?\n\nPatient: Not really disappeared, no… I mean, maybe some days feel a little quieter than others, like if the weather’s nice or I’ve slept better, but it’s been there more or less since not long after my close friend died.\n\nThat was about… a year and five months ago now, I think. Funny how I can remember that, but then I’ll forget where I put the little address book with her handwriting in it—well, anyway. It started after that time, and I don’t think it’s really gone away and come back.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been present all the time since they started, or have they come and gone?\n\nPatient: It’s been more all the time, I think… not really coming and going. Since not long after my close friend died—about a year and five months ago now—it’s just sort of stayed around.\n\nSome days are softer than others, like if I keep busy with small things, folding towels or looking through old fabric scraps… but no, I wouldn’t say it left and then came back.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble organizing your thoughts in your head, even when you're not trying to explain them to someone else?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think so. It’s like I’ll start thinking about one thing, like whether I brought in the mail, or if I was meant to call someone from church, and then somehow I’m thinking about quilt patterns—those little flying geese ones—and then birds outside, and then… warnings, or what I was supposed to be careful about.\n\nAnd when I try to say it out loud, I can see people’s faces change a little, like they’re waiting for the point and I’ve lost the thread. I don’t always notice it right away, but then I feel foolish.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned a close friend died about a year and five months ago, can you tell me more about that and how it affected you?\n\nPatient: She was… she was my closest friend, really. And after she died I just kept turning it over and over in my head, like a stone in my pocket. I think about whether I should’ve called her more, or gone by more often, or noticed something I was supposed to notice.\n\nI know people say, “You did what you could,” but I don’t feel that way. I feel like I failed her somehow. And then it spreads, you know… like if I failed her, maybe I’ve failed everybody else too. My family, people from church… even little things, like forgetting to send a card, start feeling like proof.\n\nI’m sorry, I’m getting off track. She used to like those lemon cookies from the bakery on Maple—well, I don’t know if it’s still there now. But yes… mostly it left me feeling like I did something wrong, or didn’t do enough, and I keep going back over it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your thoughts are more disorganized and harder to follow than your speech?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe the trouble is worse when I try to say it out loud. In my head it already feels sort of tangled, but when I’m explaining, I’ll start with one thing—like the mail, or someone from church I meant to call—and then suddenly I’m talking about quilt patterns, or birds outside, or… warnings, and I can tell people don’t know where I’m going with it.\n\nLike their faces get that look, you know, polite but confused. So I suppose my speech makes it more obvious. My thoughts are messy first, but talking… talking sort of shows it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

So I suppose my speech makes it more obvious. My thoughts are messy first, but talking… talking sort of shows it.\n\n[Last Question]\nYou mentioned your close friend's death, did that event trigger the start of these feelings and thoughts for you?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

Yes… I think that’s when it really started, not long after she died. It’s been about a year and five months now.

I mean, time gets a little strange when I think about it—there was the service, and then people brought casseroles, and someone had that blue dish with the cracked lid—but yes, after that. Not right that minute maybe, but soon after. That’s when these feelings and thoughts began hanging around.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF20.0, F23\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I came in because I’ve been feeling uneasy, even when things are supposed to be normal. Like at church, or with the walking club—I used to enjoy that, you know, it’s nice to get out and see people, and they have those little bulletins at church with all the announcements and birthdays and such—but lately I can’t shake the feeling that some of them are watching me.\n\nAnd not just watching, exactly… it feels like they’re putting little messages about me in what they say, or in the bulletin. I know that may sound odd, but it keeps happening in my mind, and it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or sounds that other people can't hear?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes… mostly when the house is real quiet. I’ll be in the living room, or doing dishes, and I’ll hear my name, or just a few words, like it’s my friend calling from the kitchen or out on the porch.\n\nIt sounds clear enough that I’ve actually answered back before, and then I remember nobody’s there. It’s not all the time, but it does shake me up when it happens.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Oh… I’d say it’s been going on for maybe eleven months now. Not every single minute, but the voices and those little messages, and even just trying to keep my thoughts lined up… it’s been most of this past year. I remember thinking it started around when the weather was changing, but then I get mixed up because that was when my neighbor was fixing her porch too, and I kept hearing all that hammering… but yes, about eleven months.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to express your thoughts clearly or in an orderly fashion when talking to others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I try to answer plain, but then I’ll start telling someone about the mail, because maybe there was a flyer or something from church, and then somehow I’m talking about quilt patterns, or those little birds that come to the feeder, and then—well, I lose the straight line of it.\n\nI can see it on people’s faces sometimes, like they’re waiting for me to get back to the point, and I’m not even sure what point I was making. It’s embarrassing, because in my head it feels connected, but when it comes out, it gets all tangled.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any problems with your daily activities, like cooking, cleaning, or taking care of yourself?\n\nPatient: Well, I still live at home, so I manage some things… I can make tea and toast, that sort of thing. But I’m not keeping up like I used to. I used to go to my quilting group—there’s a lady there who always brings lemon bars, and she cuts the fabric so straight it makes me jealous—but I haven’t been going much.\n\nAnd my walking club, I sort of fell away from that too. The garden’s been getting away from me, which bothers me because I always liked my flowers. My daughter’s been checking in more now, making sure I’ve eaten something decent and reminding me about appointments. I don’t like needing that, but… yes, things have slipped some.\n\nDoctor: Have you been acting in ways that seem strange or unusual to other people?\n\nPatient: Yes… maybe. I don’t mean to be odd, but there are mornings where I’ll be standing by the front door with my purse in my hand, just… standing there. Like I was going somewhere, but I don’t go out, and I can’t quite remember what I meant to do.\n\nSometimes I notice the light coming through the little window there, and I start thinking I should wash the curtain, because it gets dusty near the door, and then I’m still holding the purse. So yes, I suppose if someone saw me, it would look strange.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where you became immobile or unable to move, like being stuck in one position for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that happens some. Like I said, there are mornings I end up standing by the front door with my purse in my hand, and I’m just there for a long while. Not exactly frozen like a statue, but not moving on either.\n\nI’ll think, “Was I going to the store?” or maybe I meant to check the mail, and then I notice the rug needs shaking out, or there’s a smudge on the little glass pane, and I’m still just standing there. It’s a strange feeling, like the next step got misplaced.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to stay focused on one topic when talking or thinking?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I start trying to explain one thing, like whether I got the mail or if I meant to call someone from church, and then somehow I’m talking about quilt patterns—because the blue one really does remind me of those little birds near the feeder—and then I’ll mention the warnings I’ve been noticing, and… well, by then I can see people looking at me like they don’t know where I’m going with it.\n\nI don’t always notice it right away. I think I’m making sense, but it comes out all tangled.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your thoughts are often disjointed or difficult to follow, even when you're not talking to someone?\n\nPatient: Yes… even when I’m just sitting by myself, it can be that way. I’ll start thinking, “Did I bring the mail in?” or maybe I should call someone from church, and then somehow my mind is on the quilt in the back room, the blue pattern, and those birds outside… and then the warnings I’ve been noticing sort of come into it.\n\nIt feels connected to me at the time, but if I tried to say it out loud, people would look at me like they don’t know what I’m getting at. Sometimes I don’t know how I got from one thought to the other either.\n\nDoctor: When you're talking to someone and your thoughts seem to jump around, do you think the other person can usually understand what you're trying to say?\n\nPatient: Not usually, no… I mean, I think I’m explaining it clear enough when I start. Like I’ll be talking about the mail, or maybe someone from church I meant to call, and then it goes sideways somehow—quilt patterns, the blue one, the birds outside, and then those warnings I’ve noticed.\n\nAnd I can see it on their face after a bit. They’re listening, but they don’t really know what I’m getting at. Sometimes I don’t either, once I hear myself.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been causing you significant distress or problems in your daily life, such as at work, with friends, or with family?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has, I suppose. I still live at home, I’m not needing to be somewhere else or anything, but I don’t keep up with things like I used to.\n\nI stopped going to the quilting gatherings mostly, and the walking club too… which is a shame, because there was this one lady who always had the nicest little thermos, red with flowers on it—anyway. And my garden, I used to be out there fussing with it more, but now it’s just… not as much.\n\nMy daughter’s been checking on me more too, about whether I ate and if I remembered appointments. I don’t like feeling watched over, but I guess she’s worried.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where your symptoms seemed to disappear or get much better, only to come back later?\n\nPatient: Not really disappeared, no… I mean, maybe some days feel a little quieter than others, like if the weather’s nice or I’ve slept better, but it’s been there more or less since not long after my close friend died.\n\nThat was about… a year and five months ago now, I think. Funny how I can remember that, but then I’ll forget where I put the little address book with her handwriting in it—well, anyway. It started after that time, and I don’t think it’s really gone away and come back.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been present all the time since they started, or have they come and gone?\n\nPatient: It’s been more all the time, I think… not really coming and going. Since not long after my close friend died—about a year and five months ago now—it’s just sort of stayed around.\n\nSome days are softer than others, like if I keep busy with small things, folding towels or looking through old fabric scraps… but no, I wouldn’t say it left and then came back.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble organizing your thoughts in your head, even when you're not trying to explain them to someone else?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think so. It’s like I’ll start thinking about one thing, like whether I brought in the mail, or if I was meant to call someone from church, and then somehow I’m thinking about quilt patterns—those little flying geese ones—and then birds outside, and then… warnings, or what I was supposed to be careful about.\n\nAnd when I try to say it out loud, I can see people’s faces change a little, like they’re waiting for the point and I’ve lost the thread. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I came in because I’ve been feeling uneasy, even when things are supposed to be normal. Like I said, there are mornings I end up standing by the front door with my purse in my hand, and I’m just there for a long while. Not exactly frozen like a statue, but not moving on either.\n\nI’ll think, “Was I going to the store?” or maybe I meant to check the mail, and then I notice the rug needs shaking out, or there’s a smudge on the little glass pane, and I’m still just standing there. It’s a strange feeling, like the next step got misplaced.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to stay focused on one topic when talking or thinking?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Sometimes I don’t know how I got from one thought to the other either.\n\nDoctor: When you're talking to someone and your thoughts seem to jump around, do you think the other person can usually understand what you're trying to say?\n\nPatient: Not usually, no… I mean, I think I’m explaining it clear enough when I start. They’re listening, but they don’t really know what I’m getting at. Sometimes I don’t either, once I hear myself.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been causing you significant distress or problems in your daily life, such as at work, with friends, or with family?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has, I suppose. I don’t mean to be odd, but there are mornings where I’ll be standing by the front door with my purse in my hand, just… standing there. Like I was going somewhere, but I don’t go out, and I can’t quite remember what I meant to do.\n\nSometimes I notice the light coming through the little window there, and I start thinking I should wash the curtain, because it gets dusty near the door, and then I’m still holding the purse. So yes, I suppose if someone saw me, it would look strange.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where you became immobile or unable to move, like being stuck in one position for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that happens some. Like I said, there are mornings I end up standing by the front door with my purse in my hand, and I’m just there for a long while. Not exactly frozen like a statue, but not moving on either.\n\nI’ll think, “Was I going to the store?” or maybe I meant to check the mail, and then I notice the rug needs shaking out, or there’s a smudge on the little glass pane, and I’m still just standing there. It’s a strange feeling, like the next step got misplaced.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to stay focused on one topic when talking or thinking?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I start trying to explain one thing, like whether I got the mail or if I meant to call someone from church, and then somehow I’m talking about quilt patterns—because the blue one really does remind me of those little birds near the feeder—and then I’ll mention the warnings I’ve been noticing, and… well, by then I can see people looking at me like they don’t know where I’m going with it.\n\nI don’t always notice it right away. I think I’m making sense, but it comes out all tangled.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your thoughts are often disjointed or difficult to follow, even when you're not talking to someone?\n\nPatient: Yes… even when I’m just sitting by myself, it can be that way. I’ll start thinking, “Did I bring the mail in?” or maybe I should call someone from church, and then somehow my mind is on the quilt in the back room, the blue pattern, and those birds outside… and then the warnings I’ve been noticing sort of come into it.\n\nIt feels connected to me at the time, but if I tried to say it out loud, people would look at me like they don’t know what I’m getting at. Sometimes I don’t know how I got from one thought to the other either.\n\nDoctor: When you're talking to someone and your thoughts seem to jump around, do you think the other person can usually understand what you're trying to say?\n\nPatient: Not usually, no… I mean, I think I’m explaining it clear enough when I start. Like I’ll be talking about the mail, or maybe someone from church I meant to call, and then it goes sideways somehow—quilt patterns, the blue one, the birds outside, and then those warnings I’ve noticed.\n\nAnd I can see it on their face after a bit. They’re listening, but they don’t really know what I’m getting at. Sometimes I don’t either, once I hear myself.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been causing you significant distress or problems in your daily life, such as at work, with friends, or with family?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has, I suppose. I still live at home, I’m not needing to be somewhere else or anything, but I don’t keep up with things like I used to.\n\nI stopped going to the quilting gatherings mostly, and the walking club too… which is a shame, because there was this one lady who always had the nicest little thermos, red with flowers on it—anyway. And my garden, I used to be out there fussing with it more, but now it’s just… not as much.\n\nMy daughter’s been checking on me more too, about whether I ate and if I remembered appointments. I don’t like feeling watched over, but I guess she’s worried.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where your symptoms seemed to disappear or get much better, only to come back later?\n\nPatient: Not really disappeared, no… I mean, maybe some days feel a little quieter than others, like if the weather’s nice or I’ve slept better, but it’s been there more or less since not long after my close friend died.\n\nThat was about… a year and five months ago now, I think. Funny how I can remember that, but then I’ll forget where I put the little address book with her handwriting in it—well, anyway. It started after that time, and I don’t think it’s really gone away and come back.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been present all the time since they started, or have they come and gone?\n\nPatient: It’s been more all the time, I think… not really coming and going. Since not long after my close friend died—about a year and five months ago now—it’s just sort of stayed around.\n\nSome days are softer than others, like if I keep busy with small things, folding towels or looking through old fabric scraps… but no, I wouldn’t say it left and then came back.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble organizing your thoughts in your head, even when you're not trying to explain them to someone else?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think so. It’s like I’ll start thinking about one thing, like whether I brought in the mail, or if I was meant to call someone from church, and then somehow I’m thinking about quilt patterns—those little flying geese ones—and then birds outside, and then… warnings, or what I was supposed to be careful about.\n\nAnd when I try to say it out loud, I can see people’s faces change a little, like they’re waiting for the point and I’ve lost the thread. I don’t always notice it right away, but then I feel foolish.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned a close friend died about a year and five months ago, can you tell me more about that and how it affected you?\n\nPatient: She was… she was my closest friend, really. And after she died I just kept turning it over and over in my head, like a stone in my pocket. I think about whether I should’ve called her more, or gone by more often, or noticed something I was supposed to notice.\n\nI know people say, “You did what you could,” but I don’t feel that way. I feel like I failed her somehow. And then it spreads, you know… like if I failed her, maybe I’ve failed everybody else too. My family, people from church… even little things, like forgetting to send a card, start feeling like proof.\n\nI’m sorry, I’m getting off track. She used to like those lemon cookies from the bakery on Maple—well, I don’t know if it’s still there now. But yes… mostly it left me feeling like I did something wrong, or didn’t do enough, and I keep going back over it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your thoughts are more disorganized and harder to follow than your speech?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe the trouble is worse when I try to say it out loud. In my head it already feels sort of tangled, but when I’m explaining, I’ll start with one thing—like the mail, or someone from church I meant to call—and then suddenly I’m talking about quilt patterns, or birds outside, or… warnings, and I can tell people don’t know where I’m going with it.\n\nLike their faces get that look, you know, polite but confused. So I suppose my speech makes it more obvious. My thoughts are messy first, but talking… talking sort of shows it.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned your close friend's death, did that event trigger the start of these feelings and thoughts for you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s when it really started, not long after she died. It’s been about a year and five months now.\n\nI mean, time gets a little strange when I think about it—there was the service, and then people brought casseroles, and someone had that blue dish with the cracked lid—but yes, after that. Not right that minute maybe, but soon after. That’s when these feelings and thoughts began hanging around.\n\nDoctor: Have the voices you hear been getting louder or more prominent over time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if louder is the right word… maybe more noticeable when it’s quiet. Like at night, or if I’m in the kitchen and the house has settled—you know how the refrigerator makes that little hum, and the porch boards creak when it gets cold.\n\nSometimes I’ll hear my name, or just a few words, and it sounds like her voice. From the kitchen, or out by the porch. Clear enough that I’ll answer, just automatic, before I remember… there isn’t anybody there. Then I feel foolish, and sad, mostly.\n\nDoctor: Did the death of your close friend directly trigger the start of these unusual thoughts and feelings you've been experiencing?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’d say it did, or at least it started not long after she died. It’s been going on since then—about a year and five months, I think.\n\nI remember because it was around the colder part of the year, and then the seasons kept changing, and I kept thinking maybe it would pass when spring came, or after her birthday was over, or… well, it didn’t really. So yes, that’s when it began.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I suppose it has, though I’m still at home, still managing some things. I’m not working, so it’s not like I’ve missed a job or anything.

But I don’t really go to my quilting group anymore. I used to like that—there was a woman there who always brought lemon cookies, too dry, but everyone ate them anyway. And the walking club… I’ve mostly stopped that. My garden’s been let go more than I’d like. The weeds get tall before I even notice.

My daughter checks on me more now—meals, appointments, that sort of thing. She’ll call and ask if I ate lunch or if I remembered where I’m supposed to be. I don’t love needing that, but… yes, things have slipped some.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF20.0, F23\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I came in because I’ve been feeling uneasy, even when things are supposed to be normal. Like at church, or with the walking club—I used to enjoy that, you know, it’s nice to get out and see people, and they have those little bulletins at church with all the announcements and birthdays and such—but lately I can’t shake the feeling that some of them are watching me.\n\nAnd not just watching, exactly… it feels like they’re putting little messages about me in what they say, or in the bulletin. I know that may sound odd, but it keeps happening in my mind, and it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or sounds that other people can't hear?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes… mostly when the house is real quiet. I’ll be in the living room, or doing dishes, and I’ll hear my name, or just a few words, like it’s my friend calling from the kitchen or out on the porch.\n\nIt sounds clear enough that I’ve actually answered back before, and then I remember nobody’s there. It’s not all the time, but it does shake me up when it happens.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Oh… I’d say it’s been going on for maybe eleven months now. Not every single minute, but the voices and those little messages, and even just trying to keep my thoughts lined up… it’s been most of this past year. I remember thinking it started around when the weather was changing, but then I get mixed up because that was when my neighbor was fixing her porch too, and I kept hearing all that hammering… but yes, about eleven months.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to express your thoughts clearly or in an orderly fashion when talking to others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I try to answer plain, but then I’ll start telling someone about the mail, because maybe there was a flyer or something from church, and then somehow I’m talking about quilt patterns, or those little birds that come to the feeder, and then—well, I lose the straight line of it.\n\nI can see it on people’s faces sometimes, like they’re waiting for me to get back to the point, and I’m not even sure what point I was making. It’s embarrassing, because in my head it feels connected, but when it comes out, it gets all tangled.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any problems with your daily activities, like cooking, cleaning, or taking care of yourself?\n\nPatient: Well, I still live at home, so I manage some things… I can make tea and toast, that sort of thing. But I’m not keeping up like I used to. I used to go to my quilting group—there’s a lady there who always brings lemon bars, and she cuts the fabric so straight it makes me jealous—but I haven’t been going much.\n\nAnd my walking club, I sort of fell away from that too. The garden’s been getting away from me, which bothers me because I always liked my flowers. My daughter’s been checking in more now, making sure I’ve eaten something decent and reminding me about appointments. I don’t like needing that, but… yes, things have slipped some.\n\nDoctor: Have you been acting in ways that seem strange or unusual to other people?\n\nPatient: Yes… maybe. I don’t mean to be odd, but there are mornings where I’ll be standing by the front door with my purse in my hand, just… standing there. Like I was going somewhere, but I don’t go out, and I can’t quite remember what I meant to do.\n\nSometimes I notice the light coming through the little window there, and I start thinking I should wash the curtain, because it gets dusty near the door, and then I’m still holding the purse. So yes, I suppose if someone saw me, it would look strange.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where you became immobile or unable to move, like being stuck in one position for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that happens some. Like I said, there are mornings I end up standing by the front door with my purse in my hand, and I’m just there for a long while. Not exactly frozen like a statue, but not moving on either.\n\nI’ll think, “Was I going to the store?” or maybe I meant to check the mail, and then I notice the rug needs shaking out, or there’s a smudge on the little glass pane, and I’m still just standing there. It’s a strange feeling, like the next step got misplaced.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to stay focused on one topic when talking or thinking?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I start trying to explain one thing, like whether I got the mail or if I meant to call someone from church, and then somehow I’m talking about quilt patterns—because the blue one really does remind me of those little birds near the feeder—and then I’ll mention the warnings I’ve been noticing, and… well, by then I can see people looking at me like they don’t know where I’m going with it.\n\nI don’t always notice it right away. I think I’m making sense, but it comes out all tangled.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your thoughts are often disjointed or difficult to follow, even when you're not talking to someone?\n\nPatient: Yes… even when I’m just sitting by myself, it can be that way. I’ll start thinking, “Did I bring the mail in?” or maybe I should call someone from church, and then somehow my mind is on the quilt in the back room, the blue pattern, and those birds outside… and then the warnings I’ve been noticing sort of come into it.\n\nIt feels connected to me at the time, but if I tried to say it out loud, people would look at me like they don’t know what I’m getting at. Sometimes I don’t know how I got from one thought to the other either.\n\nDoctor: When you're talking to someone and your thoughts seem to jump around, do you think the other person can usually understand what you're trying to say?\n\nPatient: Not usually, no… I mean, I think I’m explaining it clear enough when I start. Like I’ll be talking about the mail, or maybe someone from church I meant to call, and then it goes sideways somehow—quilt patterns, the blue one, the birds outside, and then those warnings I’ve noticed.\n\nAnd I can see it on their face after a bit. They’re listening, but they don’t really know what I’m getting at. Sometimes I don’t either, once I hear myself.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been causing you significant distress or problems in your daily life, such as at work, with friends, or with family?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has, I suppose. I still live at home, I’m not needing to be somewhere else or anything, but I don’t keep up with things like I used to.\n\nI stopped going to the quilting gatherings mostly, and the walking club too… which is a shame, because there was this one lady who always had the nicest little thermos, red with flowers on it—anyway. And my garden, I used to be out there fussing with it more, but now it’s just… not as much.\n\nMy daughter’s been checking on me more too, about whether I ate and if I remembered appointments. I don’t like feeling watched over, but I guess she’s worried.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where your symptoms seemed to disappear or get much better, only to come back later?\n\nPatient: Not really disappeared, no… I mean, maybe some days feel a little quieter than others, like if the weather’s nice or I’ve slept better, but it’s been there more or less since not long after my close friend died.\n\nThat was about… a year and five months ago now, I think. Funny how I can remember that, but then I’ll forget where I put the little address book with her handwriting in it—well, anyway. It started after that time, and I don’t think it’s really gone away and come back.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been present all the time since they started, or have they come and gone?\n\nPatient: It’s been more all the time, I think… not really coming and going. Since not long after my close friend died—about a year and five months ago now—it’s just sort of stayed around.\n\nSome days are softer than others, like if I keep busy with small things, folding towels or looking through old fabric scraps… but no, I wouldn’t say it left and then came back.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble organizing your thoughts in your head, even when you're not trying to explain them to someone else?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think so. It’s like I’ll start thinking about one thing, like whether I brought in the mail, or if I was meant to call someone from church, and then somehow I’m thinking about quilt patterns—those little flying geese ones—and then birds outside, and then… warnings, or what I was supposed to be careful about.\n\nAnd when I try to say it out loud, I can see people’s faces change a little, like they’re waiting for the point and I’ve lost the thread. I don’t always notice it right away, but then I feel foolish.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned a close friend died about a year and five months ago, can you tell me more about that and how it affected you?\n\nPatient: She was… she was my closest friend, really. And after she died I just kept turning it over and over in my head, like a stone in my pocket. I think about whether I should’ve called her more, or gone by more often, or noticed something I was supposed to notice.\n\nI know people say, “You did what you could,” but I don’t feel that way. I feel like I failed her somehow. And then it spreads, you know… like if I failed her, maybe I’ve failed everybody else too. My family, people from church… even little things, like forgetting to send a card, start feeling like proof.\n\nI’m sorry, I’m getting off track. She used to like those lemon cookies from the bakery on Maple—well, I don’t know if it’s still there now. But yes… mostly it left me feeling like I did something wrong, or didn’t do enough, and I keep going back over it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your thoughts are more disorganized and harder to follow than your speech?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe the trouble is worse when I try to say it out loud. In my head it already feels sort of tangled, but when I’m explaining, I’ll start with one thing—like the mail, or someone from church I meant to call—and then suddenly I’m talking about quilt patterns, or birds outside, or… warnings, and I can tell people don’t know where I’m going with it.\n\nLike their faces get that look, you know, polite but confused. So I suppose my speech makes it more obvious. My thoughts are messy first, but talking… talking sort of shows it.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned your close friend's death, did that event trigger the start of these feelings and thoughts for you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s when it really started, not long after she died. It’s been about a year and five months now.\n\nI mean, time gets a little strange when I think about it—there was the service, and then people brought casseroles, and someone had that blue dish with the cracked lid—but yes, after that. Not right that minute maybe, but soon after. That’s when these feelings and thoughts began hanging around.\n\nDoctor: Have the voices you hear been getting louder or more prominent over time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if louder is the right word… maybe more noticeable when it’s quiet. From the kitchen, or out by the porch. Clear enough that I’ll answer, just automatic, before I remember… there isn’t anybody there. Then I feel foolish, and sad, mostly.\n\nDoctor: Did the death of your close friend directly trigger the start of these unusual thoughts and feelings you've been experiencing?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’d say it did, or at least it started not long after she died. It’s been going on since then—about a year and five months, I think.\n\nI remember because it was around the colder part of the year, and then the seasons kept changing, and I kept thinking maybe it would pass when spring came, or after her birthday was over, or… well, it didn’t really. So yes, that’s when it began.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant impairment in social or occupational functioning as a result of your symptoms?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it has, though I’m still at home, still managing some things. I don’t love needing that, but… yes, things have slipped some.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
