I don’t like feeling watched over, but I guess she’s worried.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where your symptoms seemed to disappear or get much better, only to come back later?\n\nPatient: Not really disappeared, no… I mean, maybe some days feel a little quieter than others, like if the weather’s nice or I’ve slept better, but it’s been there more or less since not long after my close friend died.\n\nThat was about… a year and five months ago now, I think. Funny how I can remember that, but then I’ll forget where I put the little address book with her handwriting in it—well, anyway. It started after that time, and I don’t think it’s really gone away and come back.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been present all the time since they started, or have they come and gone?\n\nPatient: It’s been more all the time, I think… not really coming and going. Since not long after my close friend died—about a year and five months ago now—it’s just sort of stayed around.\n\nSome days are softer than others, like if I keep busy with small things, folding towels or looking through old fabric scraps… but no, I wouldn’t say it left and then came back.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble organizing your thoughts in your head, even when you're not trying to explain them to someone else?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think so. It’s like I’ll start thinking about one thing, like whether I brought in the mail, or if I was meant to call someone from church, and then somehow I’m thinking about quilt patterns—those little flying geese ones—and then birds outside, and then… warnings, or what I was supposed to be careful about.\n\nAnd when I try to say it out loud, I can see people’s faces change a little, like they’re waiting for the point and I’ve lost the thread. I don’t always notice it right away, but then I feel foolish.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned a close friend died about a year and five months ago, can you tell me more about that and how it affected you?\n\nPatient: She was… she was my closest friend, really. And after she died I just kept turning it over and over in my head, like a stone in my pocket. I think about whether I should’ve called her more, or gone by more often, or noticed something I was supposed to notice.\n\nI know people say, “You did what you could,” but I don’t feel that way. I feel like I failed her somehow. And then it spreads, you know… like if I failed her, maybe I’ve failed everybody else too. My family, people from church… even little things, like forgetting to send a card, start feeling like proof.\n\nI’m sorry, I’m getting off track. She used to like those lemon cookies from the bakery on Maple—well, I don’t know if it’s still there now. But yes… mostly it left me feeling like I did something wrong, or didn’t do enough, and I keep going back over it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your thoughts are more disorganized and harder to follow than your speech?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe the trouble is worse when I try to say it out loud. In my head it already feels sort of tangled, but when I’m explaining, I’ll start with one thing—like the mail, or someone from church I meant to call—and then suddenly I’m talking about quilt patterns, or birds outside, or… warnings, and I can tell people don’t know where I’m going with it.\n\nLike their faces get that look, you know, polite but confused. So I suppose my speech makes it more obvious. My thoughts are messy first, but talking… talking sort of shows it.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned your close friend's death, did that event trigger the start of these feelings and thoughts for you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s when it really started, not long after she died. It’s been about a year and five months now.\n\nI mean, time gets a little strange when I think about it—there was the service, and then people brought casseroles, and someone had that blue dish with the cracked lid—but yes, after that. Not right that minute maybe, but soon after. That’s when these feelings and thoughts began hanging around.\n\nDoctor: Have the voices you hear been getting louder or more prominent over time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if louder is the right word… maybe more noticeable when it’s quiet. Like at night, or if I’m in the kitchen and the house has settled—you know how the refrigerator makes that little hum, and the porch boards creak when it gets cold.\n\nSometimes I’ll hear my name, or just a few words, and it sounds like her voice. From the kitchen, or out by the porch. Clear enough that I’ll answer, just automatic, before I remember… there isn’t anybody there. Then I feel foolish, and sad, mostly.\n\nDoctor: Did the death of your close friend directly trigger the start of these unusual thoughts and feelings you've been experiencing?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’d say it did, or at least it started not long after she died. It’s been going on since then—about a year and five months, I think.\n\nI remember because it was around the colder part of the year, and then the seasons kept changing, and I kept thinking maybe it would pass when spring came, or after her birthday was over, or… well, it didn’t really. So yes, that’s when it began.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant impairment in social or occupational functioning as a result of your symptoms?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it has, though I’m still at home, still managing some things. I’m not working, so it’s not like I’ve missed a job or anything.\n\nBut I don’t really go to my quilting group anymore. I used to like that—there was a woman there who always brought lemon cookies, too dry, but everyone ate them anyway. And the walking club… I’ve mostly stopped that. My garden’s been let go more than I’d like. The weeds get tall before I even notice.\n\nMy daughter checks on me more now—meals, appointments, that sort of thing. She’ll call and ask if I ate lunch or if I remembered where I’m supposed to be. I don’t love needing that, but… yes, things have slipped some."
  }
